People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Well… I suppose the honest answer is that I’m here because things have gotten dark in a way I can’t seem to talk myself out of anymore. I keep having this thought that my wife and the kids would be better off if I just… didn’t wake up one morning. Not that I’m proud to say that. I know it sounds terrible out loud.

And lately I’ve caught myself saying — more than once — that I don’t need to see another winter. It’s not just a phrase, either. I mean, I say it like I’m tired, but there’s more behind it than that. It’s been sitting in my head, and I guess that’s what scared me enough, or worried my family enough, to get me here.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, very much so. It’s not only bowling, though that was part of it. I used to be the kind of man who had little things on the calendar — bridge, tending the garden, helping with the cooking at church, even getting out to a Cubs game when I could. I used to look forward to those things. They gave the week some shape.

Now I mostly just sit in my chair. I’ll think, “I should go out and pull weeds,” or “Maybe I ought to call about bridge,” and then I just… don’t. It feels like there’s no point to it, or like I can’t make myself care enough to move. Even the Cubs — I used to get excited about going, about the whole day of it — and now it just feels far away from me, like something another version of me used to enjoy.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I wish I could say there were a few things that still felt good, but most of it just feels… flat.

I used to look forward to bridge, for example. I liked the routine of it, seeing the same faces, the little jokes, the way you could lose an afternoon without thinking too hard about anything else. And the garden — I used to get satisfaction from that, even just checking the tomatoes or pulling weeds. Church cooking, too. I wasn’t some great chef, but I liked being useful, being part of something. And going to Cubs games, that used to feel like a real outing, something I’d think about for days beforehand.

Now I mostly just sit in my chair and think about those things like they belong to somebody else. I know I should care. I know I used to care. But I can’t seem to make myself feel any pleasure in it, or even enough interest to get up and do it. It’s like the wanting part of me has gone quiet.

Yes… yes, I do. More than I’d like to admit.

I go over things in my head again and again, especially with him — my bowling partner — little things I said or didn’t say, times I could’ve called, or maybe noticed something, or been a better friend somehow. I know some of it probably sounds small from the outside, but in my mind it all adds up. I’ll sit there and replay conversations, and I think, “Why did I say it that way?” or “Why wasn’t I there more?” It’s like I’m looking for every place I failed him.

And it’s not only him. It’s my family too. I think about all the ways I’ve let people down over the years, things I should’ve done better, times I was impatient or selfish or useless. I keep thinking I’m just no good to anybody now. Like I’m more of a burden than a help.

Sometimes it feels like whatever bad is coming to me, I probably deserve it. I don’t say that lightly. It just sits there in my mind — that I must have earned some kind of punishment, one way or another.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… my energy is just about gone, and I’m slow with everything now. My wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and that’s about right. It feels like there’s resistance in the air somehow, like every little motion takes planning.

Getting dressed can take me so long that I’ll sit on the edge of the bed with a shirt in my hands and just… stay there. Or I’ll start across the kitchen to get something and halfway there I’m already tired of the whole idea. Not tired like after doing work, exactly — more like my body won’t answer me the way it used to.

Even simple things stretch out. Buttoning a shirt, putting on shoes, crossing from one room to another. I know it shouldn’t take that long. I can feel how slow I am. And sometimes I give up before I’ve done much of anything, because it all feels like too much effort.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose the honest answer is that I’m here because things have gotten dark in a way I can’t seem to talk myself out of anymore. I know it sounds terrible out loud.\n\nAnd lately I’ve caught myself saying — more than once — that I don’t need to see another winter. It’s not just a phrase, either. I mean, I say it like I’m tired, but there’s more behind it than that. It’s been sitting in my head, and I guess that’s what scared me enough, or worried my family enough, to get me here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year now, I suppose. It really started after my bowling partner died — that was about a year and a month ago. We’d been bowling together for years, same league, same little routines, and after he was gone something in me just sort of dropped out. At first I thought it was grief, you know, ordinary grief, because at my age you do lose people. But it didn’t lift. It just kept settling in heavier and heavier after that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy, like bowling?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, very much so. It’s not only bowling, though that was part of it. I used to be the kind of man who had little things on the calendar — bridge, tending the garden, helping with the cooking at church, even getting out to a Cubs game when I could. I used to look forward to those things. They gave the week some shape.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my chair. I’ll think, “I should go out and pull weeds,” or “Maybe I ought to call about bridge,” and then I just… don’t. It feels like there’s no point to it, or like I can’t make myself care enough to move. Even the Cubs — I used to get excited about going, about the whole day of it — and now it just feels far away from me, like something another version of me used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find any pleasure in the things you do?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wish I could say there were a few things that still felt good, but most of it just feels… flat.\n\nI used to look forward to bridge, for example. And the garden — I used to get satisfaction from that, even just checking the tomatoes or pulling weeds. Church cooking, too. I wasn’t some great chef, but I liked being useful, being part of something. And going to Cubs games, that used to feel like a real outing, something I’d think about for days beforehand.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my chair and think about those things like they belong to somebody else. I know I should care. I know I used to care. But I can’t seem to make myself feel any pleasure in it, or even enough interest to get up and do it. It’s like the wanting part of me has gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. More than I’d like to admit.\n\nI go over things in my head again and again, especially with him — my bowling partner — little things I said or didn’t say, times I could’ve called, or maybe noticed something, or been a better friend somehow. I know some of it probably sounds small from the outside, but in my mind it all adds up. I’ll sit there and replay conversations, and I think, “Why did I say it that way?” or “Why wasn’t I there more?” It’s like I’m looking for every place I failed him.\n\nAnd it’s not only him. It’s my family too. I think about all the ways I’ve let people down over the years, things I should’ve done better, times I was impatient or selfish or useless. I keep thinking I’m just no good to anybody now. Like I’m more of a burden than a help.\n\nSometimes it feels like whatever bad is coming to me, I probably deserve it. I don’t say that lightly. It just sits there in my mind — that I must have earned some kind of punishment, one way or another.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or the way you move?\n\nPatient: Yes… my energy is just about gone, and I’m slow with everything now. My wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and that’s about right. It feels like there’s resistance in the air somehow, like every little motion takes planning.\n\nGetting dressed can take me so long that I’ll sit on the edge of the bed with a shirt in my hands and just… stay there. Or I’ll start across the kitchen to get something and halfway there I’m already tired of the whole idea. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if they’re connected exactly… but it feels that way sometimes. When those guilty thoughts get going, it’s like my whole body gets heavier. I’ll be sitting there thinking about what I did wrong, or what I should’ve done, and then even standing up feels like some enormous task.

My wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and that’s really how it feels. Not just slow because I’m old — I mean slow in a way that feels… stuck. I’ll try to get dressed and it can take me forever. I’ll sit there with my clothes in my lap, knowing what the next step is, but not doing it. Or I’ll start across the kitchen and halfway through it feels like I’ve already used up whatever I had in me.

So yes, maybe the guilt is part of it. I can’t say where one ends and the other begins. But when my mind is full of those thoughts, my body seems to follow along, and before I’ve done much of anything, I just give up.

It’s been sitting in my head, and I guess that’s what scared me enough, or worried my family enough, to get me here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year now, I suppose. It really started after my bowling partner died — that was about a year and a month ago. We’d been bowling together for years, same league, same little routines, and after he was gone something in me just sort of dropped out. At first I thought it was grief, you know, ordinary grief, because at my age you do lose people. But it didn’t lift. It just kept settling in heavier and heavier after that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy, like bowling?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, very much so. It’s not only bowling, though that was part of it. I used to be the kind of man who had little things on the calendar — bridge, tending the garden, helping with the cooking at church, even getting out to a Cubs game when I could. I used to look forward to those things. They gave the week some shape.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my chair. I’ll think, “I should go out and pull weeds,” or “Maybe I ought to call about bridge,” and then I just… don’t. It feels like there’s no point to it, or like I can’t make myself care enough to move. Even the Cubs — I used to get excited about going, about the whole day of it — and now it just feels far away from me, like something another version of me used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find any pleasure in the things you do?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wish I could say there were a few things that still felt good, but most of it just feels… flat.\n\nI used to look forward to bridge, for example. I liked the routine of it, seeing the same faces, the little jokes, the way you could lose an afternoon without thinking too hard about anything else. And the garden — I used to get satisfaction from that, even just checking the tomatoes or pulling weeds. Church cooking, too. I wasn’t some great chef, but I liked being useful, being part of something. And going to Cubs games, that used to feel like a real outing, something I’d think about for days beforehand.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my chair and think about those things like they belong to somebody else. I know I should care. I know I used to care. But I can’t seem to make myself feel any pleasure in it, or even enough interest to get up and do it. It’s like the wanting part of me has gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. More than I’d like to admit.\n\nI go over things in my head again and again, especially with him — my bowling partner — little things I said or didn’t say, times I could’ve called, or maybe noticed something, or been a better friend somehow. I know some of it probably sounds small from the outside, but in my mind it all adds up. I’ll sit there and replay conversations, and I think, “Why did I say it that way?” or “Why wasn’t I there more?” It’s like I’m looking for every place I failed him.\n\nAnd it’s not only him. It’s my family too. I think about all the ways I’ve let people down over the years, things I should’ve done better, times I was impatient or selfish or useless. I keep thinking I’m just no good to anybody now. Like I’m more of a burden than a help.\n\nSometimes it feels like whatever bad is coming to me, I probably deserve it. I don’t say that lightly. It just sits there in my mind — that I must have earned some kind of punishment, one way or another.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or the way you move?\n\nPatient: Yes… my energy is just about gone, and I’m slow with everything now. My wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and that’s about right. It feels like there’s resistance in the air somehow, like every little motion takes planning.\n\nGetting dressed can take me so long that I’ll sit on the edge of the bed with a shirt in my hands and just… stay there. Or I’ll start across the kitchen to get something and halfway there I’m already tired of the whole idea. Not tired like after doing work, exactly — more like my body won’t answer me the way it used to.\n\nEven simple things stretch out. Buttoning a shirt, putting on shoes, crossing from one room to another. I know it shouldn’t take that long. I can feel how slow I am. And sometimes I give up before I’ve done much of anything, because it all feels like too much effort.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your slowed movements are connected to the guilty thoughts you've been having?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if they’re connected exactly… but it feels that way sometimes. When those guilty thoughts get going, it’s like my whole body gets heavier. I’ll be sitting there thinking about what I did wrong, or what I should’ve done, and then even standing up feels like some enormous task.\n\nMy wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and that’s really how it feels. Not just slow because I’m old — I mean slow in a way that feels… stuck. I’ll try to get dressed and it can take me forever. I’ll sit there with my clothes in my lap, knowing what the next step is, but not doing it. Or I’ll start across the kitchen and halfway through it feels like I’ve already used up whatever I had in me.\n\nSo yes, maybe the guilt is part of it. I can’t say where one ends and the other begins. But when my mind is full of those thoughts, my body seems to follow along, and before I’ve done much of anything, I just give up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"intent": "determine the presence of psychotic symptoms", "question": "Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?"}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I have. I don’t like saying that, because it sounds… it sounds like something is wrong with me in a way I can’t take back once I say it.

It’s mostly in the evenings. The house will be quiet, or I think it’s quiet, and then I’ll hear his voice — my bowling partner’s voice — plain as day. Sometimes it sounds like it’s coming from the hallway, sometimes from the spare room. Not like a memory exactly, not like when you remember someone talking. It’s more outside of me than that, like if I turned my head fast enough I might see him standing there.

And he’ll say things… things like I should have been there, or that I failed him. Sometimes it’s just a few words, but they hit me hard because it sounds so much like him. I’ll ask my wife if she heard anything, and she’ll say the house is quiet, that there’s nobody there. But I know what I heard in that moment. It feels real when it happens.

I haven’t really seen him, not in a clear way. It’s more the voice. And I wish it would stop, because when it happens I just sit there frozen, listening, feeling like maybe he’s right.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really see him, no… not like a person standing there. I’ve looked, sometimes, because the voice is so clear that I expect there to be someone in the hallway or in the spare room. But when I check, there’s nothing there.

It’s more that I hear him. Some evenings, when the house is quiet, I’ll hear my bowling partner’s voice plain as day, like it’s coming from down the hall or from that spare room. He’ll say I should have been there, or that I failed him. And I’ll ask my wife if she heard it, because it feels so real in the moment, but she says the house is quiet.

So… shadows or figures, not really. But the voice is real enough that I end up looking for him anyway.

When those guilty thoughts get going, it’s like my whole body gets heavier. I’ll be sitting there thinking about what I did wrong, or what I should’ve done, and then even standing up feels like some enormous task.\n\nMy wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and that’s really how it feels. Not just slow because I’m old — I mean slow in a way that feels… stuck. I’ll try to get dressed and it can take me forever. I’ll sit there with my clothes in my lap, knowing what the next step is, but not doing it. Or I’ll start across the kitchen and halfway through it feels like I’ve already used up whatever I had in me.\n\nSo yes, maybe the guilt is part of it. I can’t say where one ends and the other begins. But when my mind is full of those thoughts, my body seems to follow along, and before I’ve done much of anything, I just give up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I don’t like saying that, because it sounds… it sounds like something is wrong with me in a way I can’t take back once I say it.\n\nIt’s mostly in the evenings. The house will be quiet, or I think it’s quiet, and then I’ll hear his voice — my bowling partner’s voice — plain as day. Sometimes it sounds like it’s coming from the hallway, sometimes from the spare room. Not like a memory exactly, not like when you remember someone talking. It’s more outside of me than that, like if I turned my head fast enough I might see him standing there.\n\nAnd he’ll say things… things like I should have been there, or that I failed him. Sometimes it’s just a few words, but they hit me hard because it sounds so much like him. I’ll ask my wife if she heard anything, and she’ll say the house is quiet, that there’s nobody there. But I know what I heard in that moment. It feels real when it happens.\n\nI haven’t really seen him, not in a clear way. It’s more the voice. And I wish it would stop, because when it happens I just sit there frozen, listening, feeling like maybe he’s right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that you know aren't really there, like shadows or figures, when you're alone or in a quiet space?\n\nPatient: I don’t really see him, no… not like a person standing there. I’ve looked, sometimes, because the voice is so clear that I expect there to be someone in the hallway or in the spare room. But when I check, there’s nothing there.\n\nIt’s more that I hear him. Some evenings, when the house is quiet, I’ll hear my bowling partner’s voice plain as day, like it’s coming from down the hall or from that spare room. He’ll say I should have been there, or that I failed him. And I’ll ask my wife if she heard it, because it feels so real in the moment, but she says the house is quiet.\n\nSo… shadows or figures, not really. But the voice is real enough that I end up looking for him anyway.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It feels outside of me when it happens. I know that may not make sense, and afterward I can sit there and wonder if my mind is doing something to me, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like a thought I’m having.

It’s like… some evenings I’ll be sitting in the house, and I hear his voice plain as day, coming from the hallway or sometimes that spare room. Not like remembering him, not like me thinking, “Oh, he used to say this.” It sounds like him talking from somewhere else in the house. And he’ll say I should have been there, or that I failed him.

That’s what makes it so hard to dismiss. My wife will tell me the house is quiet, that she didn’t hear anything, and I believe she’s telling the truth. But when I hear it, it feels real — like it’s coming from outside me, not from inside my own head.

I know it sounds terrible out loud.\n\nAnd lately I’ve caught myself saying — more than once — that I don’t need to see another winter. It’s not just a phrase, either. It’s been sitting in my head, and I guess that’s what scared me enough, or worried my family enough, to get me here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year now, I suppose. It really started after my bowling partner died — that was about a year and a month ago. We’d been bowling together for years, same league, same little routines, and after he was gone something in me just sort of dropped out. At first I thought it was grief, you know, ordinary grief, because at my age you do lose people. But it didn’t lift. It just kept settling in heavier and heavier after that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy, like bowling?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, very much so. It’s not only bowling, though that was part of it. I used to be the kind of man who had little things on the calendar — bridge, tending the garden, helping with the cooking at church, even getting out to a Cubs game when I could. I used to look forward to those things. They gave the week some shape.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my chair. I’ll think, “I should go out and pull weeds,” or “Maybe I ought to call about bridge,” and then I just… don’t. It feels like there’s no point to it, or like I can’t make myself care enough to move. Even the Cubs — I used to get excited about going, about the whole day of it — and now it just feels far away from me, like something another version of me used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find any pleasure in the things you do?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wish I could say there were a few things that still felt good, but most of it just feels… flat.\n\nI used to look forward to bridge, for example. I liked the routine of it, seeing the same faces, the little jokes, the way you could lose an afternoon without thinking too hard about anything else. And the garden — I used to get satisfaction from that, even just checking the tomatoes or pulling weeds. Church cooking, too. I wasn’t some great chef, but I liked being useful, being part of something. And going to Cubs games, that used to feel like a real outing, something I’d think about for days beforehand.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my chair and think about those things like they belong to somebody else. I know I should care. I know I used to care. But I can’t seem to make myself feel any pleasure in it, or even enough interest to get up and do it. It’s like the wanting part of me has gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. More than I’d like to admit.\n\nI go over things in my head again and again, especially with him — my bowling partner — little things I said or didn’t say, times I could’ve called, or maybe noticed something, or been a better friend somehow. I know some of it probably sounds small from the outside, but in my mind it all adds up. I’ll sit there and replay conversations, and I think, “Why did I say it that way?” or “Why wasn’t I there more?” It’s like I’m looking for every place I failed him.\n\nAnd it’s not only him. It’s my family too. I think about all the ways I’ve let people down over the years, things I should’ve done better, times I was impatient or selfish or useless. I keep thinking I’m just no good to anybody now. Like I’m more of a burden than a help.\n\nSometimes it feels like whatever bad is coming to me, I probably deserve it. I don’t say that lightly. It just sits there in my mind — that I must have earned some kind of punishment, one way or another.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or the way you move?\n\nPatient: Yes… my energy is just about gone, and I’m slow with everything now. My wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and that’s about right. It feels like there’s resistance in the air somehow, like every little motion takes planning.\n\nGetting dressed can take me so long that I’ll sit on the edge of the bed with a shirt in my hands and just… stay there. Or I’ll start across the kitchen to get something and halfway there I’m already tired of the whole idea. Not tired like after doing work, exactly — more like my body won’t answer me the way it used to.\n\nEven simple things stretch out. Buttoning a shirt, putting on shoes, crossing from one room to another. I know it shouldn’t take that long. I can feel how slow I am. And sometimes I give up before I’ve done much of anything, because it all feels like too much effort.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your slowed movements are connected to the guilty thoughts you've been having?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if they’re connected exactly… but it feels that way sometimes. When those guilty thoughts get going, it’s like my whole body gets heavier. I’ll be sitting there thinking about what I did wrong, or what I should’ve done, and then even standing up feels like some enormous task.\n\nMy wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and that’s really how it feels. Not just slow because I’m old — I mean slow in a way that feels… stuck. I’ll try to get dressed and it can take me forever. I’ll sit there with my clothes in my lap, knowing what the next step is, but not doing it. Or I’ll start across the kitchen and halfway through it feels like I’ve already used up whatever I had in me.\n\nSo yes, maybe the guilt is part of it. I can’t say where one ends and the other begins. But when my mind is full of those thoughts, my body seems to follow along, and before I’ve done much of anything, I just give up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I don’t like saying that, because it sounds… it sounds like something is wrong with me in a way I can’t take back once I say it.\n\nIt’s mostly in the evenings. The house will be quiet, or I think it’s quiet, and then I’ll hear his voice — my bowling partner’s voice — plain as day. Sometimes it sounds like it’s coming from the hallway, sometimes from the spare room. Not like a memory exactly, not like when you remember someone talking. It’s more outside of me than that, like if I turned my head fast enough I might see him standing there.\n\nAnd he’ll say things… things like I should have been there, or that I failed him. Sometimes it’s just a few words, but they hit me hard because it sounds so much like him. I’ll ask my wife if she heard anything, and she’ll say the house is quiet, that there’s nobody there. But I know what I heard in that moment. It feels real when it happens.\n\nI haven’t really seen him, not in a clear way. It’s more the voice. And I wish it would stop, because when it happens I just sit there frozen, listening, feeling like maybe he’s right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that you know aren't really there, like shadows or figures, when you're alone or in a quiet space?\n\nPatient: I don’t really see him, no… not like a person standing there. I’ve looked, sometimes, because the voice is so clear that I expect there to be someone in the hallway or in the spare room. But when I check, there’s nothing there.\n\nIt’s more that I hear him. Some evenings, when the house is quiet, I’ll hear my bowling partner’s voice plain as day, like it’s coming from down the hall or from that spare room. He’ll say I should have been there, or that I failed him. And I’ll ask my wife if she heard it, because it feels so real in the moment, but she says the house is quiet.\n\nSo… shadows or figures, not really. But the voice is real enough that I end up looking for him anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voice you hear is a part of your own mind or something outside of you?\n\nPatient: It feels outside of me when it happens. I know that may not make sense, and afterward I can sit there and wonder if my mind is doing something to me, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like a thought I’m having.\n\nIt’s like… some evenings I’ll be sitting in the house, and I hear his voice plain as day, coming from the hallway or sometimes that spare room. Not like remembering him, not like me thinking, “Oh, he used to say this.” It sounds like him talking from somewhere else in the house. And he’ll say I should have been there, or that I failed him.\n\nThat’s what makes it so hard to dismiss. My wife will tell me the house is quiet, that she didn’t hear anything, and I believe she’s telling the truth. But when I hear it, it feels real — like it’s coming from outside me, not from inside my own head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F33.1", "F33.2", "F32.2"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

And I wish it would stop, because when it happens I just sit there frozen, listening, feeling like maybe he’s right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that you know aren't really there, like shadows or figures, when you're alone or in a quiet space?\n\nPatient: I don’t really see him, no… not like a person standing there. I’ve looked, sometimes, because the voice is so clear that I expect there to be someone in the hallway or in the spare room. But when I check, there’s nothing there.\n\nIt’s more that I hear him. Some evenings, when the house is quiet, I’ll hear my bowling partner’s voice plain as day, like it’s coming from down the hall or from that spare room. He’ll say I should have been there, or that I failed him. And I’ll ask my wife if she heard it, because it feels so real in the moment, but she says the house is quiet.\n\nSo… shadows or figures, not really. But the voice is real enough that I end up looking for him anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voice you hear is a part of your own mind or something outside of you?\n\nPatient: It feels outside of me when it happens. I know that may not make sense, and afterward I can sit there and wonder if my mind is doing something to me, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like a thought I’m having.\n\nIt’s like… some evenings I’ll be sitting in the house, and I hear his voice plain as day, coming from the hallway or sometimes that spare room. Not like remembering him, not like me thinking, “Oh, he used to say this.” It sounds like him talking from somewhere else in the house. And he’ll say I should have been there, or that I failed him.\n\nThat’s what makes it so hard to dismiss. My wife will tell me the house is quiet, that she didn’t hear anything, and I believe she’s telling the truth. But when I hear it, it feels real — like it’s coming from outside me, not from inside my own head.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're moving slower than you used to, or is it more that you just can't seem to get started on things?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 80 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Psychomotor_Changes\nmanifestation: \"My wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and even getting dressed or crossing the kitchen takes me so long that I give up before I’ve done much of anything.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s both, I think… but the slowing down is the part my wife notices the most. She says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and that’s exactly what it feels like sometimes. Like my legs work, but there’s so much weight in them that every step takes thinking about.

Even little things take me forever now. Getting dressed, just putting on a shirt or finding my socks, I’ll sit on the edge of the bed and stare at them like it’s some big job. Crossing the kitchen to get something feels like more than it should. I’ll start to do something and then halfway through I just… stop. Not because I decided to stop, exactly. More like I run out before I’ve done much of anything.

And then when I think about all the steps, even simple ones, I can’t seem to get started. So yes, I’m slower, but it’s also like there’s no push in me anymore. My wife has to wait on me, and I can tell she’s trying not to show frustration, but I know it must be hard to watch.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.1, F33.2, F32.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose the honest answer is that I’m here because things have gotten dark in a way I can’t seem to talk myself out of anymore. I keep having this thought that my wife and the kids would be better off if I just… didn’t wake up one morning. Not that I’m proud to say that. I know it sounds terrible out loud.\n\nAnd lately I’ve caught myself saying — more than once — that I don’t need to see another winter. It’s not just a phrase, either. I mean, I say it like I’m tired, but there’s more behind it than that. It’s been sitting in my head, and I guess that’s what scared me enough, or worried my family enough, to get me here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year now, I suppose. It really started after my bowling partner died — that was about a year and a month ago. We’d been bowling together for years, same league, same little routines, and after he was gone something in me just sort of dropped out. At first I thought it was grief, you know, ordinary grief, because at my age you do lose people. But it didn’t lift. It just kept settling in heavier and heavier after that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy, like bowling?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, very much so. It’s not only bowling, though that was part of it. I used to be the kind of man who had little things on the calendar — bridge, tending the garden, helping with the cooking at church, even getting out to a Cubs game when I could. I used to look forward to those things. They gave the week some shape.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my chair. I’ll think, “I should go out and pull weeds,” or “Maybe I ought to call about bridge,” and then I just… don’t. It feels like there’s no point to it, or like I can’t make myself care enough to move. Even the Cubs — I used to get excited about going, about the whole day of it — and now it just feels far away from me, like something another version of me used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find any pleasure in the things you do?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wish I could say there were a few things that still felt good, but most of it just feels… flat.\n\nI used to look forward to bridge, for example. I liked the routine of it, seeing the same faces, the little jokes, the way you could lose an afternoon without thinking too hard about anything else. And the garden — I used to get satisfaction from that, even just checking the tomatoes or pulling weeds. Church cooking, too. I wasn’t some great chef, but I liked being useful, being part of something. And going to Cubs games, that used to feel like a real outing, something I’d think about for days beforehand.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my chair and think about those things like they belong to somebody else. I know I should care. I know I used to care. But I can’t seem to make myself feel any pleasure in it, or even enough interest to get up and do it. It’s like the wanting part of me has gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. More than I’d like to admit.\n\nI go over things in my head again and again, especially with him — my bowling partner — little things I said or didn’t say, times I could’ve called, or maybe noticed something, or been a better friend somehow. I know some of it probably sounds small from the outside, but in my mind it all adds up. I’ll sit there and replay conversations, and I think, “Why did I say it that way?” or “Why wasn’t I there more?” It’s like I’m looking for every place I failed him.\n\nAnd it’s not only him. It’s my family too. I think about all the ways I’ve let people down over the years, things I should’ve done better, times I was impatient or selfish or useless. I keep thinking I’m just no good to anybody now. Like I’m more of a burden than a help.\n\nSometimes it feels like whatever bad is coming to me, I probably deserve it. I don’t say that lightly. It just sits there in my mind — that I must have earned some kind of punishment, one way or another.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or the way you move?\n\nPatient: Yes… my energy is just about gone, and I’m slow with everything now. My wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and that’s about right. It feels like there’s resistance in the air somehow, like every little motion takes planning.\n\nGetting dressed can take me so long that I’ll sit on the edge of the bed with a shirt in my hands and just… stay there. Or I’ll start across the kitchen to get something and halfway there I’m already tired of the whole idea. Not tired like after doing work, exactly — more like my body won’t answer me the way it used to.\n\nEven simple things stretch out. Buttoning a shirt, putting on shoes, crossing from one room to another. I know it shouldn’t take that long. I can feel how slow I am. And sometimes I give up before I’ve done much of anything, because it all feels like too much effort.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your slowed movements are connected to the guilty thoughts you've been having?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if they’re connected exactly… but it feels that way sometimes. When those guilty thoughts get going, it’s like my whole body gets heavier. I’ll be sitting there thinking about what I did wrong, or what I should’ve done, and then even standing up feels like some enormous task.\n\nMy wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and that’s really how it feels. Not just slow because I’m old — I mean slow in a way that feels… stuck. I’ll try to get dressed and it can take me forever. I’ll sit there with my clothes in my lap, knowing what the next step is, but not doing it. Or I’ll start across the kitchen and halfway through it feels like I’ve already used up whatever I had in me.\n\nSo yes, maybe the guilt is part of it. I can’t say where one ends and the other begins. But when my mind is full of those thoughts, my body seems to follow along, and before I’ve done much of anything, I just give up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I don’t like saying that, because it sounds… it sounds like something is wrong with me in a way I can’t take back once I say it.\n\nIt’s mostly in the evenings. The house will be quiet, or I think it’s quiet, and then I’ll hear his voice — my bowling partner’s voice — plain as day. Sometimes it sounds like it’s coming from the hallway, sometimes from the spare room. Not like a memory exactly, not like when you remember someone talking. It’s more outside of me than that, like if I turned my head fast enough I might see him standing there.\n\nAnd he’ll say things… things like I should have been there, or that I failed him. Sometimes it’s just a few words, but they hit me hard because it sounds so much like him. I’ll ask my wife if she heard anything, and she’ll say the house is quiet, that there’s nobody there. But I know what I heard in that moment. It feels real when it happens.\n\nI haven’t really seen him, not in a clear way. It’s more the voice. And I wish it would stop, because when it happens I just sit there frozen, listening, feeling like maybe he’s right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that you know aren't really there, like shadows or figures, when you're alone or in a quiet space?\n\nPatient: I don’t really see him, no… not like a person standing there. ---------- turn 12 | patient:alignment | patient.build_alignment_messages() | model=gpt-5.5 ----------

[
  {

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Major Depressive Disorder with Psychotic Features. My family and pastor got scared enough by what I was saying that they pushed me to get urgent help.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since my bowling partner died, about a year and a month ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 80 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S032\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that you know aren't really there, like shadows or figures, when you're alone or in a quiet space?\n\nPatient: I don’t really see him, no… not like a person standing there. I’ve looked, sometimes, because the voice is so clear that I expect there to be someone in the hallway or in the spare room. But when I check, there’s nothing there.\n\nIt’s more that I hear him. Some evenings, when the house is quiet, I’ll hear my bowling partner’s voice plain as day, like it’s coming from down the hall or from that spare room. He’ll say I should have been there, or that I failed him. And I’ll ask my wife if she heard it, because it feels so real in the moment, but she says the house is quiet.\n\nSo… shadows or figures, not really. But the voice is real enough that I end up looking for him anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voice you hear is a part of your own mind or something outside of you?\n\nPatient: It feels outside of me when it happens. I know that may not make sense, and afterward I can sit there and wonder if my mind is doing something to me, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like a thought I’m having.\n\nIt’s like… some evenings I’ll be sitting in the house, and I hear his voice plain as day, coming from the hallway or sometimes that spare room. Not like remembering him, not like me thinking, “Oh, he used to say this.” It sounds like him talking from somewhere else in the house. And he’ll say I should have been there, or that I failed him.\n\nThat’s what makes it so hard to dismiss. My wife will tell me the house is quiet, that she didn’t hear anything, and I believe she’s telling the truth. But when I hear it, it feels real — like it’s coming from outside me, not from inside my own head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to, or is it more that you just can't seem to get started on things?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I think… but the slowing down is the part my wife notices the most. She says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and that’s exactly what it feels like sometimes. Like my legs work, but there’s so much weight in them that every step takes thinking about.\n\nEven little things take me forever now. Getting dressed, just putting on a shirt or finding my socks, I’ll sit on the edge of the bed and stare at them like it’s some big job. Crossing the kitchen to get something feels like more than it should. I’ll start to do something and then halfway through I just… stop. Not because I decided to stop, exactly. More like I run out before I’ve done much of anything.\n\nAnd then when I think about all the steps, even simple ones, I can’t seem to get started. So yes, I’m slower, but it’s also like there’s no push in me anymore. My wife has to wait on me, and I can tell she’s trying not to show frustration, but I know it must be hard to watch.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think the voice you hear is trying to tell you something specific about yourself or your actions?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. It feels like he’s telling me something about what I did, or what I didn’t do. It’s not just random noise or like hearing my name once. When I hear him in the evenings, it’s very clear, like he’s down the hall or in that spare room, and it’s always that same kind of thing — that I should have been there, that I failed him.

And I know my wife says the house is quiet. I’ll ask her, because it feels so real that I think surely she must have heard it too, but she hasn’t. Still, when it happens, it feels like the voice is aimed right at me. Like he’s accusing me, I guess. Like he wants me to know I let him down.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.1, F33.2, F32.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose the honest answer is that I’m here because things have gotten dark in a way I can’t seem to talk myself out of anymore. I keep having this thought that my wife and the kids would be better off if I just… didn’t wake up one morning. Not that I’m proud to say that. I know it sounds terrible out loud.\n\nAnd lately I’ve caught myself saying — more than once — that I don’t need to see another winter. It’s not just a phrase, either. I mean, I say it like I’m tired, but there’s more behind it than that. It’s been sitting in my head, and I guess that’s what scared me enough, or worried my family enough, to get me here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year now, I suppose. It really started after my bowling partner died — that was about a year and a month ago. We’d been bowling together for years, same league, same little routines, and after he was gone something in me just sort of dropped out. At first I thought it was grief, you know, ordinary grief, because at my age you do lose people. But it didn’t lift. It just kept settling in heavier and heavier after that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy, like bowling?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, very much so. It’s not only bowling, though that was part of it. I used to be the kind of man who had little things on the calendar — bridge, tending the garden, helping with the cooking at church, even getting out to a Cubs game when I could. I used to look forward to those things. They gave the week some shape.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my chair. I’ll think, “I should go out and pull weeds,” or “Maybe I ought to call about bridge,” and then I just… don’t. It feels like there’s no point to it, or like I can’t make myself care enough to move. Even the Cubs — I used to get excited about going, about the whole day of it — and now it just feels far away from me, like something another version of me used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find any pleasure in the things you do?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wish I could say there were a few things that still felt good, but most of it just feels… flat.\n\nI used to look forward to bridge, for example. I liked the routine of it, seeing the same faces, the little jokes, the way you could lose an afternoon without thinking too hard about anything else. And the garden — I used to get satisfaction from that, even just checking the tomatoes or pulling weeds. Church cooking, too. I wasn’t some great chef, but I liked being useful, being part of something. And going to Cubs games, that used to feel like a real outing, something I’d think about for days beforehand.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my chair and think about those things like they belong to somebody else. I know I should care. I know I used to care. But I can’t seem to make myself feel any pleasure in it, or even enough interest to get up and do it. It’s like the wanting part of me has gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. More than I’d like to admit.\n\nI go over things in my head again and again, especially with him — my bowling partner — little things I said or didn’t say, times I could’ve called, or maybe noticed something, or been a better friend somehow. I know some of it probably sounds small from the outside, but in my mind it all adds up. I’ll sit there and replay conversations, and I think, “Why did I say it that way?” or “Why wasn’t I there more?” It’s like I’m looking for every place I failed him.\n\nAnd it’s not only him. It’s my family too. I think about all the ways I’ve let people down over the years, things I should’ve done better, times I was impatient or selfish or useless. I keep thinking I’m just no good to anybody now. Like I’m more of a burden than a help.\n\nSometimes it feels like whatever bad is coming to me, I probably deserve it. I don’t say that lightly. It just sits there in my mind — that I must have earned some kind of punishment, one way or another.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or the way you move?\n\nPatient: Yes… my energy is just about gone, and I’m slow with everything now. My wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and that’s about right. It feels like there’s resistance in the air somehow, like every little motion takes planning.\n\nGetting dressed can take me so long that I’ll sit on the edge of the bed with a shirt in my hands and just… stay there. Or I’ll start across the kitchen to get something and halfway there I’m already tired of the whole idea. Not tired like after doing work, exactly — more like my body won’t answer me the way it used to.\n\nEven simple things stretch out. Buttoning a shirt, putting on shoes, crossing from one room to another. I know it shouldn’t take that long. I can feel how slow I am. And sometimes I give up before I’ve done much of anything, because it all feels like too much effort.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your slowed movements are connected to the guilty thoughts you've been having?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if they’re connected exactly… but it feels that way sometimes. When those guilty thoughts get going, it’s like my whole body gets heavier. I’ll be sitting there thinking about what I did wrong, or what I should’ve done, and then even standing up feels like some enormous task.\n\nMy wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and that’s really how it feels. Not just slow because I’m old — I mean slow in a way that feels… stuck. I’ll try to get dressed and it can take me forever. I’ll sit there with my clothes in my lap, knowing what the next step is, but not doing it. Or I’ll start across the kitchen and halfway through it feels like I’ve already used up whatever I had in me.\n\nSo yes, maybe the guilt is part of it. I can’t say where one ends and the other begins. But when my mind is full of those thoughts, my body seems to follow along, and before I’ve done much of anything, I just give up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I don’t like saying that, because it sounds… it sounds like something is wrong with me in a way I can’t take back once I say it.\n\nIt’s mostly in the evenings. The house will be quiet, or I think it’s quiet, and then I’ll hear his voice — my bowling partner’s voice — plain as day. Sometimes it sounds like it’s coming from the hallway, sometimes from the spare room. Not like a memory exactly, not like when you remember someone talking. It’s more outside of me than that, like if I turned my head fast enough I might see him standing there.\n\nAnd he’ll say things… things like I should have been there, or that I failed him. Sometimes it’s just a few words, but they hit me hard because it sounds so much like him. I’ll ask my wife if she heard anything, and she’ll say the house is quiet, that there’s nobody there. But I know what I heard in that moment. It feels real when it happens.\n\nI haven’t really seen him, not in a clear way. It’s more the voice. And I wish it would stop, because when it happens I just sit there frozen, listening, feeling like maybe he’s right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that you know aren't really there, like shadows or figures, when you're alone or in a quiet space?\n\nPatient: I don’t really see him, no… not like a person standing there. I’ve looked, sometimes, because the voice is so clear that I expect there to be someone in the hallway or in the spare room. But when I check, there’s nothing there.\n\nIt’s more that I hear him. Some evenings, when the house is quiet, I’ll hear my bowling partner’s voice plain as day, like it’s coming from down the hall or from that spare room. He’ll say I should have been there, or that I failed him. And I’ll ask my wife if she heard it, because it feels so real in the moment, but she says the house is quiet.\n\nSo… shadows or figures, not really. But the voice is real enough that I end up looking for him anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voice you hear is a part of your own mind or something outside of you?\n\nPatient: It feels outside of me when it happens. I know that may not make sense, and afterward I can sit there and wonder if my mind is doing something to me, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like a thought I’m having.\n\nIt’s like… some evenings I’ll be sitting in the house, and I hear his voice plain as day, coming from the hallway or sometimes that spare room. Not like remembering him, not like me thinking, “Oh, he used to say this.” It sounds like him talking from somewhere else in the house. And he’ll say I should have been there, or that I failed him.\n\nThat’s what makes it so hard to dismiss. My wife will tell me the house is quiet, that she didn’t hear anything, and I believe she’s telling the truth. But when I hear it, it feels real — like it’s coming from outside me, not from inside my own head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to, or is it more that you just can't seem to get started on things?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I think… but the slowing down is the part my wife notices the most. Getting dressed, just putting on a shirt or finding my socks, I’ll sit on the edge of the bed and stare at them like it’s some big job. Crossing the kitchen to get something feels like more than it should. I’ll start to do something and then halfway through I just… stop. Not because I decided to stop, exactly. More like I run out before I’ve done much of anything.\n\nAnd then when I think about all the steps, even simple ones, I can’t seem to get started. So yes, I’m slower, but it’s also like there’s no push in me anymore. My wife has to wait on me, and I can tell she’s trying not to show frustration, but I know it must be hard to watch.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voice you hear is trying to tell you something specific about yourself or your actions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It feels like he’s telling me something about what I did, or what I didn’t do. It’s not just random noise or like hearing my name once. I know I should care. I know I used to care. But I can’t seem to make myself feel any pleasure in it, or even enough interest to get up and do it. It’s like the wanting part of me has gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. More than I’d like to admit.\n\nI go over things in my head again and again, especially with him — my bowling partner — little things I said or didn’t say, times I could’ve called, or maybe noticed something, or been a better friend somehow. I know some of it probably sounds small from the outside, but in my mind it all adds up. I’ll sit there and replay conversations, and I think, “Why did I say it that way?” or “Why wasn’t I there more?” It’s like I’m looking for every place I failed him.\n\nAnd it’s not only him. It’s my family too. I think about all the ways I’ve let people down over the years, things I should’ve done better, times I was impatient or selfish or useless. I keep thinking I’m just no good to anybody now. Like I’m more of a burden than a help.\n\nSometimes it feels like whatever bad is coming to me, I probably deserve it. I don’t say that lightly. It just sits there in my mind — that I must have earned some kind of punishment, one way or another.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or the way you move?\n\nPatient: Yes… my energy is just about gone, and I’m slow with everything now. My wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and that’s about right. It feels like there’s resistance in the air somehow, like every little motion takes planning.\n\nGetting dressed can take me so long that I’ll sit on the edge of the bed with a shirt in my hands and just… stay there. Or I’ll start across the kitchen to get something and halfway there I’m already tired of the whole idea. Not tired like after doing work, exactly — more like my body won’t answer me the way it used to.\n\nEven simple things stretch out. Buttoning a shirt, putting on shoes, crossing from one room to another. I know it shouldn’t take that long. I can feel how slow I am. And sometimes I give up before I’ve done much of anything, because it all feels like too much effort.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your slowed movements are connected to the guilty thoughts you've been having?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if they’re connected exactly… but it feels that way sometimes. When those guilty thoughts get going, it’s like my whole body gets heavier. I’ll be sitting there thinking about what I did wrong, or what I should’ve done, and then even standing up feels like some enormous task.\n\nMy wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and that’s really how it feels. Not just slow because I’m old — I mean slow in a way that feels… stuck. I’ll try to get dressed and it can take me forever. I’ll sit there with my clothes in my lap, knowing what the next step is, but not doing it. Or I’ll start across the kitchen and halfway through it feels like I’ve already used up whatever I had in me.\n\nSo yes, maybe the guilt is part of it. I can’t say where one ends and the other begins. But when my mind is full of those thoughts, my body seems to follow along, and before I’ve done much of anything, I just give up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I don’t like saying that, because it sounds… it sounds like something is wrong with me in a way I can’t take back once I say it.\n\nIt’s mostly in the evenings. The house will be quiet, or I think it’s quiet, and then I’ll hear his voice — my bowling partner’s voice — plain as day. Sometimes it sounds like it’s coming from the hallway, sometimes from the spare room. Not like a memory exactly, not like when you remember someone talking. It’s more outside of me than that, like if I turned my head fast enough I might see him standing there.\n\nAnd he’ll say things… things like I should have been there, or that I failed him. Sometimes it’s just a few words, but they hit me hard because it sounds so much like him. I’ll ask my wife if she heard anything, and she’ll say the house is quiet, that there’s nobody there. But I know what I heard in that moment. It feels real when it happens.\n\nI haven’t really seen him, not in a clear way. It’s more the voice. And I wish it would stop, because when it happens I just sit there frozen, listening, feeling like maybe he’s right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that you know aren't really there, like shadows or figures, when you're alone or in a quiet space?\n\nPatient: I don’t really see him, no… not like a person standing there. I’ve looked, sometimes, because the voice is so clear that I expect there to be someone in the hallway or in the spare room. But when I check, there’s nothing there.\n\nIt’s more that I hear him. Some evenings, when the house is quiet, I’ll hear my bowling partner’s voice plain as day, like it’s coming from down the hall or from that spare room. He’ll say I should have been there, or that I failed him. And I’ll ask my wife if she heard it, because it feels so real in the moment, but she says the house is quiet.\n\nSo… shadows or figures, not really. But the voice is real enough that I end up looking for him anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voice you hear is a part of your own mind or something outside of you?\n\nPatient: It feels outside of me when it happens. I know that may not make sense, and afterward I can sit there and wonder if my mind is doing something to me, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like a thought I’m having.\n\nIt’s like… some evenings I’ll be sitting in the house, and I hear his voice plain as day, coming from the hallway or sometimes that spare room. And he’ll say I should have been there, or that I failed him.\n\nThat’s what makes it so hard to dismiss. My wife will tell me the house is quiet, that she didn’t hear anything, and I believe she’s telling the truth. But when I hear it, it feels real — like it’s coming from outside me, not from inside my own head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to, or is it more that you just can't seem to get started on things?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I think… but the slowing down is the part my wife notices the most. Crossing the kitchen to get something feels like more than it should. I’ll start to do something and then halfway through I just… stop. Not because I decided to stop, exactly. So yes, I’m slower, but it’s also like there’s no push in me anymore. It’s… more like a wish, I think. I haven’t sat down and made some detailed plan, if that’s what you mean. But the thought is there a lot, and it frightens me to say it out loud.

What brings me in, really, is I keep thinking my wife and the kids would be better off if I just didn’t wake up. Like if I went to sleep and that was the end of it, they wouldn’t have to keep watching me like this or carrying me along. And lately I’ve caught myself saying, more than once, that I don’t need to see another winter. I don’t say it like a joke, either. I mean it when I say it.

So no, I haven’t worked out some exact way to do it. It’s more that I don’t want to keep going, and I keep wishing it would just happen on its own.
