It all sort of runs together.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: I’m not sure. I haven’t been able to pick out a dependable time of day where it’s worse.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. And I wish it would stop, because when it happens I just sit there frozen, listening, feeling like maybe he’s right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that you know aren't really there, like shadows or figures, when you're alone or in a quiet space?\n\nPatient: I don’t really see him, no… not like a person standing there. I’ve looked, sometimes, because the voice is so clear that I expect there to be someone in the hallway or in the spare room. But when I check, there’s nothing there.\n\nIt’s more that I hear him. Some evenings, when the house is quiet, I’ll hear my bowling partner’s voice plain as day, like it’s coming from down the hall or from that spare room. He’ll say I should have been there, or that I failed him. And I’ll ask my wife if she heard it, because it feels so real in the moment, but she says the house is quiet.\n\nSo… shadows or figures, not really. But the voice is real enough that I end up looking for him anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voice you hear is a part of your own mind or something outside of you?\n\nPatient: It feels outside of me when it happens. I know that may not make sense, and afterward I can sit there and wonder if my mind is doing something to me, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like a thought I’m having.\n\nIt’s like… some evenings I’ll be sitting in the house, and I hear his voice plain as day, coming from the hallway or sometimes that spare room. Not like remembering him, not like me thinking, “Oh, he used to say this.” It sounds like him talking from somewhere else in the house. And he’ll say I should have been there, or that I failed him.\n\nThat’s what makes it so hard to dismiss. My wife will tell me the house is quiet, that she didn’t hear anything, and I believe she’s telling the truth. But when I hear it, it feels real — like it’s coming from outside me, not from inside my own head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to, or is it more that you just can't seem to get started on things?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I think… but the slowing down is the part my wife notices the most. She says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and that’s exactly what it feels like sometimes. Like my legs work, but there’s so much weight in them that every step takes thinking about.\n\nEven little things take me forever now. Getting dressed, just putting on a shirt or finding my socks, I’ll sit on the edge of the bed and stare at them like it’s some big job. Crossing the kitchen to get something feels like more than it should. I’ll start to do something and then halfway through I just… stop. Not because I decided to stop, exactly. More like I run out before I’ve done much of anything.\n\nAnd then when I think about all the steps, even simple ones, I can’t seem to get started. So yes, I’m slower, but it’s also like there’s no push in me anymore. My wife has to wait on me, and I can tell she’s trying not to show frustration, but I know it must be hard to watch.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voice you hear is trying to tell you something specific about yourself or your actions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It feels like he’s telling me something about what I did, or what I didn’t do. It’s not just random noise or like hearing my name once. When I hear him in the evenings, it’s very clear, like he’s down the hall or in that spare room, and it’s always that same kind of thing — that I should have been there, that I failed him.\n\nAnd I know my wife says the house is quiet. I’ll ask her, because it feels so real that I think surely she must have heard it too, but she hasn’t. Still, when it happens, it feels like the voice is aimed right at me. Like he’s accusing me, I guess. Like he wants me to know I let him down.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would carry out the act of not waking up, or is it more of a vague wish?\n\nPatient: It’s… more like a wish, I think. I haven’t sat down and made some detailed plan, if that’s what you mean. But the thought is there a lot, and it frightens me to say it out loud.\n\nWhat brings me in, really, is I keep thinking my wife and the kids would be better off if I just didn’t wake up. Like if I went to sleep and that was the end of it, they wouldn’t have to keep watching me like this or carrying me along. And lately I’ve caught myself saying, more than once, that I don’t need to see another winter. I don’t say it like a joke, either. I mean it when I say it.\n\nSo no, I haven’t worked out some exact way to do it. It’s more that I don’t want to keep going, and I keep wishing it would just happen on its own.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not really… no. I try to think of something, because I don’t want to sound like I’m saying absolutely nothing in my life matters, but the things that used to make me feel like myself just don’t do anything now.\n\nI used to look forward to bridge. I’d check who was coming, think about the hands afterward, tease people a little if they overbid. Now if someone mentions it, I just feel tired before I’ve even gone. The garden was the same way — I used to fuss over the tomatoes and the soil and all that, probably more than I needed to. Now I can look right out at it and it’s like I’m looking at somebody else’s yard. I know I should care, but I don’t feel it.\n\nEven church cooking… I used to like helping with that. There was always noise and people asking where the trays were, and I liked being useful. And Cubs games, too — going to a game used to be a real treat for me. I’d look forward to it for days. Now I just sit in my chair, and even if the game is on, it’s just colors moving around on the television. I can’t make myself care who wins.\n\nSo… no, I can’t really say there’s much pleasure in anything right now. I mostly just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a burden to your family most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, yes. I hate saying that, because my family would probably tell me I’m not, or they’d try to reassure me, but I don’t really believe it when they say it.\n\nI keep going over everything in my head, over and over — little things, old things, things I said wrong, things I didn’t do, ways I should have been better. Especially with him, but with my wife and the rest of my family too. It’s like my mind won’t let go of any of it. I’ll sit there and replay some moment from years ago and think, “There, that’s who you really are. You failed them.” And then it just piles up until I feel like I’m no good to anybody.\n\nI feel like they’re all having to work around me now, watch what they say, worry about me, carry the weight of me. And I don’t see what I’m giving back. I just feel like I’ve caused hurt and disappointment, and now they’re stuck with me like this.\n\nSometimes it feels like whatever bad thing is coming to me, I probably deserve it. Like punishment, I guess. I know that sounds awful, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this low and disconnected from things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about a year now. A little more, actually. Since my bowling partner died — that was about a year and a month ago, if I’m counting right.\n\nI don’t mean it was all perfectly fine one day and then gone the next, but that’s when it really started settling over me. After he died, things just kind of… lost their shape. I kept thinking I’d come back around, that maybe it was just grief and time would loosen it up, but it hasn’t. It’s been there since then, this heaviness and not caring about the things I used to care about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worse in the morning or if anything makes you feel a little better throughout the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I’ve tried to think about that, whether there’s a pattern to it, but I don’t know that I can say mornings are clearly worse or evenings are clearly better.\n\nSome mornings I wake up and it’s already there before I’ve even done anything, like I’m carrying it from the night before. But then other times the day just sort of stays the same all the way through. I’ll think, “Maybe after lunch I’ll feel a little more human,” or “Maybe if I sit outside for a bit it’ll lift,” but I can’t honestly say I notice much difference.\n\nThere might be little moments where I’m distracted for a minute, if someone is talking to me or there’s something I have to answer, but it doesn’t feel like better exactly. More like I’m pulled away from it for a second, and then it’s still sitting there when I come back. So… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed anything reliable that makes it ease up during the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worse in the mornings?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say that, no. I mean… some mornings are bad, certainly, but I can’t tell if they’re worse than the rest of the day in any consistent way.\n\nSometimes I wake up and it’s just immediately there, like I haven’t had any break from it at all. But then other days it’s not so much “morning” as just… the whole day has that same weight to it. I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern where mornings are always the worst and then it lifts later. If it does change, it’s subtle enough that I can’t put my finger on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your depression is worse in the mornings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that for certain. I know I already kind of answered that, but… no, I haven’t noticed a clear morning pattern.\n\nIt’s more that when I wake up, if it’s there, it feels like it’s been waiting for me. But I wouldn’t want to say mornings are definitely worse, because sometimes the afternoon feels just as heavy, and sometimes the evening doesn’t feel any different either. It all sort of runs together.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: I’m not sure. I haven’t been able to pick out a dependable time of day where it’s worse.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m sorry, I know you’ve asked it a couple times and I’m probably not giving you the clean answer you’re looking for, but I really don’t think I can say I’m specifically worse in the mornings.

It’s… some mornings are awful, yes, but not in a way where I can say, “Oh, mornings are the bad time and then it improves.” It doesn’t seem that organized. Sometimes I wake up and I feel like I’m already sunk before the day even starts, but then it may just stay that way. Other times I don’t notice much difference between morning and afternoon at all.

So if you’re asking whether there’s a definite morning-worse pattern, I haven’t noticed one. It’s more like the whole day can have the same heaviness to it, and I can’t reliably separate it by time.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.1, F33.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose the honest answer is that I’m here because things have gotten dark in a way I can’t seem to talk myself out of anymore. I keep having this thought that my wife and the kids would be better off if I just… didn’t wake up one morning. Not that I’m proud to say that. I know it sounds terrible out loud.\n\nAnd lately I’ve caught myself saying — more than once — that I don’t need to see another winter. It’s not just a phrase, either. It really started after my bowling partner died — that was about a year and a month ago. We’d been bowling together for years, same league, same little routines, and after he was gone something in me just sort of dropped out. At first I thought it was grief, you know, ordinary grief, because at my age you do lose people. But it didn’t lift. It just kept settling in heavier and heavier after that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy, like bowling?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, very much so. It’s not only bowling, though that was part of it. I used to be the kind of man who had little things on the calendar — bridge, tending the garden, helping with the cooking at church, even getting out to a Cubs game when I could. I used to look forward to those things. They gave the week some shape.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my chair. I wish I could say there were a few things that still felt good, but most of it just feels… flat.\n\nI used to look forward to bridge, for example. I liked the routine of it, seeing the same faces, the little jokes, the way you could lose an afternoon without thinking too hard about anything else. And the garden — I used to get satisfaction from that, even just checking the tomatoes or pulling weeds. Church cooking, too. I wasn’t some great chef, but I liked being useful, being part of something. And going to Cubs games, that used to feel like a real outing, something I’d think about for days beforehand.\n\nNow I mostly just sit in my chair and think about those things like they belong to somebody else. I know I should care. I know I used to care. But I can’t seem to make myself feel any pleasure in it, or even enough interest to get up and do it. It’s like the wanting part of me has gone quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. More than I’d like to admit.\n\nI go over things in my head again and again, especially with him — my bowling partner — little things I said or didn’t say, times I could’ve called, or maybe noticed something, or been a better friend somehow. I know some of it probably sounds small from the outside, but in my mind it all adds up. I’ll sit there and replay conversations, and I think, “Why did I say it that way?” or “Why wasn’t I there more?” It’s like I’m looking for every place I failed him.\n\nAnd it’s not only him. It’s my family too. I think about all the ways I’ve let people down over the years, things I should’ve done better, times I was impatient or selfish or useless. I keep thinking I’m just no good to anybody now. Like I’m more of a burden than a help.\n\nSometimes it feels like whatever bad is coming to me, I probably deserve it. I don’t say that lightly. It just sits there in my mind — that I must have earned some kind of punishment, one way or another.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or the way you move?\n\nPatient: Yes… my energy is just about gone, and I’m slow with everything now. My wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and that’s about right. It feels like there’s resistance in the air somehow, like every little motion takes planning.\n\nGetting dressed can take me so long that I’ll sit on the edge of the bed with a shirt in my hands and just… stay there. Or I’ll start across the kitchen to get something and halfway there I’m already tired of the whole idea. Not tired like after doing work, exactly — more like my body won’t answer me the way it used to.\n\nEven simple things stretch out. Buttoning a shirt, putting on shoes, crossing from one room to another. I know it shouldn’t take that long. I can feel how slow I am. And sometimes I give up before I’ve done much of anything, because it all feels like too much effort.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your slowed movements are connected to the guilty thoughts you've been having?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if they’re connected exactly… but it feels that way sometimes. When those guilty thoughts get going, it’s like my whole body gets heavier. I’ll be sitting there thinking about what I did wrong, or what I should’ve done, and then even standing up feels like some enormous task.\n\nMy wife says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and that’s really how it feels. Not just slow because I’m old — I mean slow in a way that feels… stuck. I’ll try to get dressed and it can take me forever. I’ll sit there with my clothes in my lap, knowing what the next step is, but not doing it. Or I’ll start across the kitchen and halfway through it feels like I’ve already used up whatever I had in me.\n\nSo yes, maybe the guilt is part of it. I can’t say where one ends and the other begins. But when my mind is full of those thoughts, my body seems to follow along, and before I’ve done much of anything, I just give up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I don’t like saying that, because it sounds… it sounds like something is wrong with me in a way I can’t take back once I say it.\n\nIt’s mostly in the evenings. The house will be quiet, or I think it’s quiet, and then I’ll hear his voice — my bowling partner’s voice — plain as day. Sometimes it sounds like it’s coming from the hallway, sometimes from the spare room. Not like a memory exactly, not like when you remember someone talking. It’s more outside of me than that, like if I turned my head fast enough I might see him standing there.\n\nAnd he’ll say things… things like I should have been there, or that I failed him. Sometimes it’s just a few words, but they hit me hard because it sounds so much like him. I’ll ask my wife if she heard anything, and she’ll say the house is quiet, that there’s nobody there. But I know what I heard in that moment. It feels real when it happens.\n\nI haven’t really seen him, not in a clear way. It’s more the voice. And I wish it would stop, because when it happens I just sit there frozen, listening, feeling like maybe he’s right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that you know aren't really there, like shadows or figures, when you're alone or in a quiet space?\n\nPatient: I don’t really see him, no… not like a person standing there. I’ve looked, sometimes, because the voice is so clear that I expect there to be someone in the hallway or in the spare room. But when I check, there’s nothing there.\n\nIt’s more that I hear him. Some evenings, when the house is quiet, I’ll hear my bowling partner’s voice plain as day, like it’s coming from down the hall or from that spare room. He’ll say I should have been there, or that I failed him. And I’ll ask my wife if she heard it, because it feels so real in the moment, but she says the house is quiet.\n\nSo… shadows or figures, not really. But the voice is real enough that I end up looking for him anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voice you hear is a part of your own mind or something outside of you?\n\nPatient: It feels outside of me when it happens. I know that may not make sense, and afterward I can sit there and wonder if my mind is doing something to me, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like a thought I’m having.\n\nIt’s like… some evenings I’ll be sitting in the house, and I hear his voice plain as day, coming from the hallway or sometimes that spare room. Not like remembering him, not like me thinking, “Oh, he used to say this.” It sounds like him talking from somewhere else in the house. And he’ll say I should have been there, or that I failed him.\n\nThat’s what makes it so hard to dismiss. My wife will tell me the house is quiet, that she didn’t hear anything, and I believe she’s telling the truth. But when I hear it, it feels real — like it’s coming from outside me, not from inside my own head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to, or is it more that you just can't seem to get started on things?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I think… but the slowing down is the part my wife notices the most. She says I move like I’m walking through deep snow, and that’s exactly what it feels like sometimes. Like my legs work, but there’s so much weight in them that every step takes thinking about.\n\nEven little things take me forever now. Getting dressed, just putting on a shirt or finding my socks, I’ll sit on the edge of the bed and stare at them like it’s some big job. Crossing the kitchen to get something feels like more than it should. I’ll start to do something and then halfway through I just… stop. Not because I decided to stop, exactly. More like I run out before I’ve done much of anything.\n\nAnd then when I think about all the steps, even simple ones, I can’t seem to get started. So yes, I’m slower, but it’s also like there’s no push in me anymore. My wife has to wait on me, and I can tell she’s trying not to show frustration, but I know it must be hard to watch.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voice you hear is trying to tell you something specific about yourself or your actions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It feels like he’s telling me something about what I did, or what I didn’t do. It’s not just random noise or like hearing my name once. When I hear him in the evenings, it’s very clear, like he’s down the hall or in that spare room, and it’s always that same kind of thing — that I should have been there, that I failed him.\n\nAnd I know my wife says the house is quiet. I’ll ask her, because it feels so real that I think surely she must have heard it too, but she hasn’t. Still, when it happens, it feels like the voice is aimed right at me. Like he’s accusing me, I guess. Like he wants me to know I let him down.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would carry out the act of not waking up, or is it more of a vague wish?\n\nPatient: It’s… more like a wish, I think. I haven’t sat down and made some detailed plan, if that’s what you mean. But the thought is there a lot, and it frightens me to say it out loud.\n\nWhat brings me in, really, is I keep thinking my wife and the kids would be better off if I just didn’t wake up. Like if I went to sleep and that was the end of it, they wouldn’t have to keep watching me like this or carrying me along. And lately I’ve caught myself saying, more than once, that I don’t need to see another winter. I don’t say it like a joke, either. I mean it when I say it.\n\nSo no, I haven’t worked out some exact way to do it. It’s more that I don’t want to keep going, and I keep wishing it would just happen on its own.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not really… no. I try to think of something, because I don’t want to sound like I’m saying absolutely nothing in my life matters, but the things that used to make me feel like myself just don’t do anything now.\n\nI used to look forward to bridge. I’d check who was coming, think about the hands afterward, tease people a little if they overbid. Now if someone mentions it, I just feel tired before I’ve even gone. The garden was the same way — I used to fuss over the tomatoes and the soil and all that, probably more than I needed to. Now I can look right out at it and it’s like I’m looking at somebody else’s yard. I know I should care, but I don’t feel it.\n\nEven church cooking… I used to like helping with that. There was always noise and people asking where the trays were, and I liked being useful. And Cubs games, too — going to a game used to be a real treat for me. I’d look forward to it for days. Now I just sit in my chair, and even if the game is on, it’s just colors moving around on the television. I can’t make myself care who wins.\n\nSo… no, I can’t really say there’s much pleasure in anything right now. I mostly just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a burden to your family most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, yes. I hate saying that, because my family would probably tell me I’m not, or they’d try to reassure me, but I don’t really believe it when they say it.\n\nI keep going over everything in my head, over and over — little things, old things, things I said wrong, things I didn’t do, ways I should have been better. Especially with him, but with my wife and the rest of my family too. It’s like my mind won’t let go of any of it. I’ll sit there and replay some moment from years ago and think, “There, that’s who you really are. You failed them.” And then it just piles up until I feel like I’m no good to anybody.\n\nI feel like they’re all having to work around me now, watch what they say, worry about me, carry the weight of me. And I don’t see what I’m giving back. I just feel like I’ve caused hurt and disappointment, and now they’re stuck with me like this.\n\nSometimes it feels like whatever bad thing is coming to me, I probably deserve it. Like punishment, I guess. I know that sounds awful, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this low and disconnected from things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about a year now. A little more, actually. Since my bowling partner died — that was about a year and a month ago, if I’m counting right.\n\nI don’t mean it was all perfectly fine one day and then gone the next, but that’s when it really started settling over me. After he died, things just kind of… lost their shape. I kept thinking I’d come back around, that maybe it was just grief and time would loosen it up, but it hasn’t. It’s been there since then, this heaviness and not caring about the things I used to care about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worse in the morning or if anything makes you feel a little better throughout the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I’ve tried to think about that, whether there’s a pattern to it, but I don’t know that I can say mornings are clearly worse or evenings are clearly better.\n\nSome mornings I wake up and it’s already there before I’ve even done anything, like I’m carrying it from the night before. But then other times the day just sort of stays the same all the way through. I’ll think, “Maybe after lunch I’ll feel a little more human,” or “Maybe if I sit outside for a bit it’ll lift,” but I can’t honestly say I notice much difference.\n\nThere might be little moments where I’m distracted for a minute, if someone is talking to me or there’s something I have to answer, but it doesn’t feel like better exactly. More like I’m pulled away from it for a second, and then it’s still sitting there when I come back. So… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed anything reliable that makes it ease up during the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worse in the mornings?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say that, no. I mean… some mornings are bad, certainly, but I can’t tell if they’re worse than the rest of the day in any consistent way.\n\nSometimes I wake up and it’s just immediately there, like I haven’t had any break from it at all. But then other days it’s not so much “morning” as just… the whole day has that same weight to it. I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern where mornings are always the worst and then it lifts later. If it does change, it’s subtle enough that I can’t put my finger on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your depression is worse in the mornings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that for certain. I know I already kind of answered that, but… no, I haven’t noticed a clear morning pattern.\n\nIt’s more that when I wake up, if it’s there, it feels like it’s been waiting for me. But I wouldn’t want to say mornings are definitely worse, because sometimes the afternoon feels just as heavy, and sometimes the evening doesn’t feel any different either. It all sort of runs together.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: I’m not sure. I haven’t been able to pick out a dependable time of day where it’s worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worse in the mornings?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’ve asked it a couple times and I’m probably not giving you the clean answer you’re looking for, but I really don’t think I can say I’m specifically worse in the mornings.\n\nIt’s… some mornings are awful, yes, but not in a way where I can say, “Oh, mornings are the bad time and then it improves.” It doesn’t seem that organized. Sometimes I wake up and I feel like I’m already sunk before the day even starts, but then it may just stay that way. Other times I don’t notice much difference between morning and afternoon at all.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether there’s a definite morning-worse pattern, I haven’t noticed one. It’s more like the whole day can have the same heaviness to it, and I can’t reliably separate it by time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. You failed them.” And then it just piles up until I feel like I’m no good to anybody.\n\nI feel like they’re all having to work around me now, watch what they say, worry about me, carry the weight of me. And I don’t see what I’m giving back. I just feel like I’ve caused hurt and disappointment, and now they’re stuck with me like this.\n\nSometimes it feels like whatever bad thing is coming to me, I probably deserve it. Like punishment, I guess. I know that sounds awful, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this low and disconnected from things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about a year now. A little more, actually. Since my bowling partner died — that was about a year and a month ago, if I’m counting right.\n\nI don’t mean it was all perfectly fine one day and then gone the next, but that’s when it really started settling over me. After he died, things just kind of… lost their shape. I kept thinking I’d come back around, that maybe it was just grief and time would loosen it up, but it hasn’t. It’s been there since then, this heaviness and not caring about the things I used to care about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worse in the morning or if anything makes you feel a little better throughout the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I’ve tried to think about that, whether there’s a pattern to it, but I don’t know that I can say mornings are clearly worse or evenings are clearly better.\n\nSome mornings I wake up and it’s already there before I’ve even done anything, like I’m carrying it from the night before. But then other times the day just sort of stays the same all the way through. I’ll think, “Maybe after lunch I’ll feel a little more human,” or “Maybe if I sit outside for a bit it’ll lift,” but I can’t honestly say I notice much difference.\n\nThere might be little moments where I’m distracted for a minute, if someone is talking to me or there’s something I have to answer, but it doesn’t feel like better exactly. More like I’m pulled away from it for a second, and then it’s still sitting there when I come back. So… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed anything reliable that makes it ease up during the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worse in the mornings?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say that, no. I mean… some mornings are bad, certainly, but I can’t tell if they’re worse than the rest of the day in any consistent way.\n\nSometimes I wake up and it’s just immediately there, like I haven’t had any break from it at all. But then other days it’s not so much “morning” as just… the whole day has that same weight to it. I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern where mornings are always the worst and then it lifts later. If it does change, it’s subtle enough that I can’t put my finger on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your depression is worse in the mornings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that for certain. I know I already kind of answered that, but… no, I haven’t noticed a clear morning pattern.\n\nIt’s more that when I wake up, if it’s there, it feels like it’s been waiting for me. But I wouldn’t want to say mornings are definitely worse, because sometimes the afternoon feels just as heavy, and sometimes the evening doesn’t feel any different either. It all sort of runs together.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is: I’m not sure. I haven’t been able to pick out a dependable time of day where it’s worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worse in the mornings?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’ve asked it a couple times and I’m probably not giving you the clean answer you’re looking for, but I really don’t think I can say I’m specifically worse in the mornings.\n\nIt’s… some mornings are awful, yes, but not in a way where I can say, “Oh, mornings are the bad time and then it improves.” It doesn’t seem that organized. Sometimes I wake up and I feel like I’m already sunk before the day even starts, but then it may just stay that way. Other times I don’t notice much difference between morning and afternoon at all.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether there’s a definite morning-worse pattern, I haven’t noticed one. It’s more like the whole day can have the same heaviness to it, and I can’t reliably separate it by time."
